Well… honestly, I came in because I keep getting up when I’m supposed to stay put, and it’s gotten noticeable enough that I can’t really brush it off anymore. Like during client interviews for work, I’ll be sitting there and I know I’m supposed to just listen and stay seated, but I’ll find myself standing up, pacing a little, going to get water, adjusting something across the room—anything but just sitting there.

Like, I can get frustrated when I feel boxed in or interrupted or like I have to sit still longer than I can tolerate, but I don’t know if that’s a mood episode or just me getting aggravated in the moment. It doesn’t feel like some big shift where I become a completely different person.\n\nSo… I guess the honest answer is I haven’t really noticed periods of my mood being unusually high, and I’m not totally sure how to judge the irritability part.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you feel the urge to get up, is it because you feel anxious or on edge?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 49 / private_detective_or_investigator / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I’d call it anxiety, exactly. I mean, I do feel uncomfortable when I’m expected to stay seated, but it doesn’t usually feel like I’m scared or worried about something specific. It’s more like this internal pressure to move, like my body is saying, “Okay, we’re done sitting now,” even if my brain knows the situation is not done.

Sometimes I do get a little tense because I’m aware I shouldn’t be getting up—like in a professional setting, I know it looks odd—so then I’m thinking about that too. But the urge itself doesn’t seem to start with me feeling anxious. It’s more physical, if that makes sense. Like restlessness before I’ve even had time to attach a worry to it.

Yes, definitely. I mean, that’s actually one of the things that makes me think it’s not new.

My grade-school report cards were always some version of, “She’s bright, but she can’t stay in her seat,” or “has trouble waiting her turn.” I remember my mother saving a few of those, and she still jokes about it now—she says I treated every classroom like a crime scene I had to pace through, which, given what I do now, is apparently very funny to her.

But yes, as a kid I was the one getting up when I wasn’t supposed to, wandering around, needing to move when everyone else seemed able to just sit there. It wasn’t something that started recently.

I’m not sure how to answer that one cleanly. I mean, yes, my mind drifts sometimes, but I don’t know if it’s more than what other people experience.

With reading, for example, it depends a lot on what I’m reading. If it’s something dry or procedural, I can get to the bottom of a page and realize I technically looked at every word but did not absorb a single thing. But if it’s something that catches me, I can stay with it. Conversations are harder to judge, because in my work I do listen carefully, but there are definitely times where someone is talking and I have to kind of pull myself back and think, “Wait, what did they just say?”

But I don’t know if I’d describe it as always having trouble sustaining attention. It’s more uneven than that. Sometimes I’m very focused, and other times my brain just slips sideways before I notice it.

Like restlessness before I’ve even had time to attach a worry to it.\n\nDoctor: Did you have this same problem with needing to get up and move around when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. I mean, that’s actually one of the things that makes me think it’s not new.\n\nMy grade-school report cards were always some version of, “She’s bright, but she can’t stay in her seat,” or “has trouble waiting her turn.” I remember my mother saving a few of those, and she still jokes about it now—she says I treated every classroom like a crime scene I had to pace through, which, given what I do now, is apparently very funny to her.\n\nBut yes, as a kid I was the one getting up when I wasn’t supposed to, wandering around, needing to move when everyone else seemed able to just sit there. It wasn’t something that started recently.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble sustaining attention, like your mind drifting when you’re supposed to focus on reading, conversations, or work?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that one cleanly. I mean, yes, my mind drifts sometimes, but I don’t know if it’s more than what other people experience.\n\nWith reading, for example, it depends a lot on what I’m reading. If it’s something dry or procedural, I can get to the bottom of a page and realize I technically looked at every word but did not absorb a single thing. But if it’s something that catches me, I can stay with it. Conversations are harder to judge, because in my work I do listen carefully, but there are definitely times where someone is talking and I have to kind of pull myself back and think, “Wait, what did they just say?”\n\nBut I don’t know if I’d describe it as always having trouble sustaining attention. It’s more uneven than that. Sometimes I’m very focused, and other times my brain just slips sideways before I notice it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I’m not sure I’d say that’s one of my main problems, at least not in a way that jumps out to me.

I mean, “organizing tasks” can mean a few different things. In my work, I do have to keep track of details, so I tend to rely on lists or notes or whatever system is in front of me, but I don’t know that I experience that as this constant inability to organize things. It’s more that if you asked me, “Is that the thing that causes you the most trouble?” I’d probably say no, not compared with the physical restlessness and needing to move.

I may have normal lapses—forgetting something unless I write it down, or having to double-check what I planned to do next—but I don’t know if that’s beyond what other people do. So I guess my answer is: I’m not really sure, and I haven’t noticed it as a major pattern the way I have with some of the other things.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well… honestly, I came in because I keep getting up when I’m supposed to stay put, and it’s gotten noticeable enough that I can’t really brush it off anymore. Like during client interviews for work, I’ll be sitting there and I know I’m supposed to just listen and stay seated, but I’ll find myself standing up, pacing a little, going to get water, adjusting something across the room—anything but just sitting there.\n\nAnd it’s not just work. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know that I’d say “worrying a lot” is the main thing for me. I mean, I worry about normal things—work, money, whether I handled something the right way, family stuff, the usual background noise of being a person with responsibilities—but I’m not sure I’m walking around with this constant free-floating worry when there’s no clear reason.

Sometimes my mind is busy, but I wouldn’t automatically call that worry. It’s more like I’m thinking through possibilities or replaying things, especially if something feels unresolved. In my line of work, you do get used to considering what could go wrong or what you might have missed, so it can be hard for me to separate “worrying” from just… mentally checking the angles.

But if you mean do I often feel anxious or worried out of nowhere, with no obvious trigger, I don’t think that’s something I’ve really noticed as a big pattern.

Sometimes I’m very focused, and other times my brain just slips sideways before I notice it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble organizing tasks or keeping track of what you need to do?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that’s one of my main problems, at least not in a way that jumps out to me.\n\nI mean, “organizing tasks” can mean a few different things. In my work, I do have to keep track of details, so I tend to rely on lists or notes or whatever system is in front of me, but I don’t know that I experience that as this constant inability to organize things. It’s more that if you asked me, “Is that the thing that causes you the most trouble?” I’d probably say no, not compared with the physical restlessness and needing to move.\n\nI may have normal lapses—forgetting something unless I write it down, or having to double-check what I planned to do next—but I don’t know if that’s beyond what other people do. So I guess my answer is: I’m not really sure, and I haven’t noticed it as a major pattern the way I have with some of the other things.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying a lot, even when there isn’t a clear reason to?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say “worrying a lot” is the main thing for me. I mean, I worry about normal things—work, money, whether I handled something the right way, family stuff, the usual background noise of being a person with responsibilities—but I’m not sure I’m walking around with this constant free-floating worry when there’s no clear reason.\n\nSometimes my mind is busy, but I wouldn’t automatically call that worry. It’s more like I’m thinking through possibilities or replaying things, especially if something feels unresolved. In my line of work, you do get used to considering what could go wrong or what you might have missed, so it can be hard for me to separate “worrying” from just… mentally checking the angles.\n\nBut if you mean do I often feel anxious or worried out of nowhere, with no obvious trigger, I don’t think that’s something I’ve really noticed as a big pattern."
  }
]

Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry. Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort. Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nUm… I’m not sure. I don’t think I’ve had anything where I felt unusually high, like euphoric or like I was on top of the world or anything that stood out to me that way.\n\nI do get irritable sometimes, but I’m not sure it’s “unusually” irritable in the way you mean. Like, I can get frustrated when I feel boxed in or interrupted or like I have to sit still longer than I can tolerate, but I don’t know if that’s a mood episode or just me getting aggravated in the moment. It doesn’t feel like some big shift where I become a completely different person.\n\nSo… I guess the honest answer is I haven’t really noticed periods of my mood being unusually high, and I’m not totally sure how to judge the irritability part.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"

Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings). Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nI’m not sure I’d say that’s one of my main problems, at least not in a way that jumps out to me.\n\nI mean, “organizing tasks” can mean a few different things. It’s more that if you asked me, “Is that the thing that causes you the most trouble?” I’d probably say no, not compared with the physical restlessness and needing to move.\n\nI may have normal lapses—forgetting something unless I write it down, or having to double-check what I planned to do next—but I don’t know if that’s beyond what other people do.
